Reading the Analects Through Self-as-an-End · Part IV

Part IV · What Was Built in His Name
解论语 · 鉴别

Han Qin (秦汉) · Independent Researcher · 2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.22656242 · Full PDF on Zenodo · CC BY 4.0

Separating the traces of his working from what was later erected

What this Part is doing

The first three volumes reconstructed a way of teaching. This one sorts what is left.

The last five books of the Analects are of visibly different character from the first fifteen. Book 16 runs on numbered lists — three friendships, three pleasures, three faults, three cautions, three fears, nine considerations. Book 18 is largely encounters with recluses and a roster of worthies. Book 20 has three chapters, and the first has no the Master said in it at all.

This is where nearly everything this book opposes is concentrated. Of the four things it opposes — ranking people, making words unquestionable, giving an unwilling book a lineage, drawing a line around a class of person — the material is almost entirely here.

And the first thing to say is how little of it there is.

1. How small the opposition is

Four things, and the passages are countable on two hands:
Ranking people16.9, 17.3
Making words unquestionable16.8 (two of its three items)
A lineage for the book20.1
Drawing a line around a class17.25, 8.20 (one clause)

Six passages, out of roughly five hundred.

This matters for how the book should be read. The claim is not that the Analects is full of doctrine. It is that a very small number of passages, in a text that mostly runs the other way, were later made to carry an enormous amount.

And they are opposed on a single ground, which is worth stating once:

All four are attempts to turn something that was alive — fitted to a person, revisable, dependent on the other party's own judgment — into something fixed: a rank, an authority, a lineage, a boundary.

What is opposed is not the content of any one of them. It is that operation.

2. Difference, and what does not follow from it

Two of the four concern ranking, and this reading's position on ranking needs to be stated precisely, because it is easy to get backwards.

This book does not claim that people are alike.

Differences are real — in aptitude, in temperament, in how far someone has got. The whole method depends on their being real: fitting the teaching to the person is necessary only if people differ. If everyone were the same, the two opposite answers to one question (11.22), the three answers about ren (12.1–12.3) and the four about filial conduct (2.5–2.8) would be pointless.

Difference is the precondition of cultivation, not an obstacle to it.

What cannot follow from a descriptive difference is:

  • a fixed rank in what a person is;
  • a lower standing as an end;
  • an unteachable status;
  • or a warrant to stop teaching.

Difference calls for fitting. It does not license ranking.

And this is what 17.2 is doing — by nature close together; by practice far apart. "Close" is not "identical." The claim is that people are close in what makes them people, and that this commonality precedes any ranking derived from their differences. The second clause says practice shapes and widens difference; it does not assert that all difference originates after birth.

Read this way, in teaching, no categories (15.39) does not depend on people being equally endowed. Teaching is open to all not because everyone starts level, but because a difference is not a reason for exclusion.

3. The lineage

20.1 strings Yao to Shun to Yu to Tang to King Wu, and hands down a formula.

And what this reading opposes is not the transmission of the passage but a use of it: reading it as an exclusive line of succession, by which whoever stands in the line has authority and whoever does not is outside.

Because of what a lineage does to a test. Once there is a line, the question shifts from is this right to who transmitted it. And that runs directly against the sentence this reading treats as decisive:

"A person can enlarge the way. It is not the way that enlarges the person." （人能弘道，非道弘人。）— 15.29

The way does not confer standing on those who hold it. There is no authority to be derived from being in possession of it — which forecloses lineage, transmission, and the whole apparatus of orthodoxy at once.

And the man himself: he transmitted and did not compose (7.1); took apart "Heaven's endowment" when a student offered it (9.6); declined sagehood (7.33); said his way was threaded on one thing and would not say what (15.3, 4.15); and told his students not to defer to him on what mattered most (15.36).

He is a poor candidate for the head of a lineage, and 20.1 supplies one.

4. Two things about how the last books are read here

First: the difference in character is registered without a claim about origins.

Numbered lists, set-piece speeches, the honorific Confucius said where the earlier books have the Master said, historical commentary, a compendium of worthies — these are observations about the character of the material. They are not findings about who wrote it or when. This book does no textual criticism and makes no such finding.

Second: non-anchoring here carries no criticism in most cases.

Book 16's numbered sets are not opposed. Three friendships that help and three that harm; nine things to consider — the content is largely sound, and several of the items are excellent. What they cannot do is serve as evidence of a way of teaching: there is no one being taught in them.

The distinction is between this is wrong and this cannot carry the reconstruction, and almost everything in these books is the second.

5. And the last chapter of the Analects

The book ends:

"Without knowing what is decreed, one has no way to be a noble person. Without knowing ritual, one has no way to stand. Without knowing words, one has no way to know people." — 20.3

And this reading judges it non-anchoring — not because the content is wrong (all three have support elsewhere) but because of what it is: a summary in three clauses, with no one being addressed and nothing at stake, at the end of a book otherwise made of exchanges.

Which is a fitting place to stop. The Analects ends with a compendium — three necessary conditions, stated to nobody in particular.

And the material this book anchors on ends earlier, with a blind musician being told where the steps are (15.42), and a question after a stable fire (10.17).

That gap is what this Part is about.

Appendix · The chapters of Books 16–20

Every chapter, with its position and the reason for it.

Reading the entries. Anchor = this reading builds on it; (core) = load-bearing. Non-anchor is not a claim that a passage is wrong — in this Part it usually means the material is compiled rather than transacted, and most of it is not opposed at all. Set aside as contested = the sense cannot be settled. Split judgment = items of one chapter judged separately.

Book 16 · 季氏 Ji Shi

The most visibly different book in the Analects. Six of its fourteen chapters are numbered sets — three friendships, three pleasures, three faults, three cautions, three fears, nine considerations. Two more are historical commentary. One is a page of ritual protocol with no speaker at all. And the honorific Confucius said appears throughout, where the earlier books have the Master said.

Nothing here anchors except one chapter — and that one is decisive (16.13, on whether his own son received private teaching).

Two of this book's four oppositions are in this book. And most of what does not anchor here is not opposed at all — the numbered sets contain a good deal of sound observation. They simply have no one in them.

16.1

The Ji family were about to attack Zhuanyu. Ran You and Jilu came to Confucius and said: "The Ji are going to take action against Zhuanyu." Confucius said: "Qiu! Is this not your fault? … Zhou Ren had a saying: 'He who can put forth his strength, let him take his place in the ranks; he who cannot, let him stand down.' … When a tiger or wild ox escapes from its cage, when a tortoiseshell or jade is ruined in its case — whose fault is it? … I fear the trouble of the Jisun lies not in Zhuanyu but within their own walls."

季氏将伐颛臾……孔子曰：「求！无乃尔是过与？……虎兕出于柙，龟玉毁于椟中，是谁之过与？……吾恐季孙之忧，不在颛臾，而在萧墙之内也。」

Non-anchor, with content recorded.

Three things about its character. It is the longest sustained argument in the book — a rebuke, a citation of ancient charge, a geographical brief, a refutation of Ran You's excuse, a quoted authority, an image, and a closing prediction. His manner elsewhere is one clause, an image left unexplained, a question that leaves the work to the hearer. The address is Confucius said, three times in one chapter. And the citations of authority are dense in a way found nowhere else.

Three things in it are worth keeping.

Not the scarcity but the inequality; not the poverty but the insecurity (不患寡而患不均，不患贫而患不安) — the endpoint is the people's security (with 12.7, 12.9).

When those far off do not submit, cultivate culture and virtue to bring them; and having come, make them secure — not force (with 13.16, 12.19, 15.1).

And the closing prediction, which was borne out: the Ji family's trouble came from inside their own house.

A likely mechanism, offered as such: several separate sayings — on inequality, on bringing the distant, on where the danger lay — assembled under one narrative occasion, with the argumentative connective tissue supplied.

And what is questioned is the assembly, not the completeness. (An earlier version of this work held that a fuller setting means less remainder. That inference was withdrawn — restoring an occasion generally prevents a prescription being mistaken for a universal law. What is noted here is the joining, not the fullness.)

16.2

Confucius said: "When the way prevails in the empire, rites, music and punitive expeditions proceed from the Son of Heaven. When it does not, they proceed from the feudal lords. … When the way prevails in the empire, government is not in the hands of the great officers. When the way prevails in the empire, the common people do not discuss it."

孔子曰：「天下有道，则礼乐征伐自天子出……天下有道，则庶人不议。」

Non-anchor — and the last clause needs comment.

The body is a historical deduction — ten generations, five, three — in the manner of 8.20 rather than of anything in Books 1–15.

And the closing clause, the common people do not discuss it, admits two readings.

Read descriptively — when things go well, people have nothing to complain of — it is unremarkable.

Read prescriptively — under good government, commoners are not permitted to discuss it — it collides with the whole reconstruction: with 8.9 as read here, with a people without trust cannot stand (12.7), and above all with 13.15, where a ruler no one dares contradict is what ruins a state.

We do not settle which was meant. What we oppose is the second reading — and note that it is the exact inversion of 13.15: what that chapter names as ruin, this clause, so read, would name as the mark of good order.

16.3

Confucius said: "It is five generations since the revenues left the ducal house, and four since government reached the great officers. Hence the descendants of the three Huan families are reduced."

孔子曰：「禄之去公室五世矣，政逮于大夫四世矣，故夫三桓之子孙微矣。」

Non-anchor.

Historical reckoning and a verdict, with no addressee and nothing being taught. (The history may well be right; the form is commentary, not a record of exchange.)

16.4

Confucius said: "There are three kinds of friend that benefit and three that harm. Friendship with the upright, with the sincere, with the widely informed — these benefit. Friendship with the plausible, with the ingratiating, with the glib — these harm."

孔子曰：「益者三友，损者三友。友直，友谅，友多闻，益矣；友便辟，友善柔，友便佞，损矣。」

Non-anchor — and not opposed.

The content is sound and consistent. The three that benefit all have substance behind them — straightness, sincerity, breadth. The three that harm are all manner without substance (with 1.3, 15.27).

What it cannot do is anchor. A numbered set, complete in itself, with no one asking and no one being answered. Nothing is left for anyone to work out, and no situation is being addressed.

This is the commonest kind of entry in this book, and it carries no criticism.

16.5

Confucius said: "There are three kinds of pleasure that benefit and three that harm. To take pleasure in the regulation of rites and music, in speaking of what is good in others, in having many worthy friends — these benefit. To take pleasure in arrogant indulgence, in idle wandering, in feasting — these harm."

孔子曰：「益者三乐，损者三乐……」

Non-anchor.

The same template as 16.4, one chapter later, with the slot filled differently — three-that-benefit, three-that-harm, friends in one, pleasures in the other.

And the repetition of a mould is itself an observation about the material (recorded, not used as proof of anything): when two adjacent chapters run the identical frame, the frame is more likely the compiler's than the occasion's.

The content again is unobjectionable — taking pleasure in speaking of what is good in others is 12.16 in another form.

16.6

Confucius said: "In attendance on a noble person there are three errors: to speak before being spoken to is called rashness; to be spoken to and not speak is called concealment; to speak without observing his expression is called blindness."

孔子曰：「侍于君子有三愆：言未及之而言谓之躁，言及之而不言谓之隐，未见颜色而言谓之瞽。」

Non-anchor — and this one is worth pausing on, because the content is excellent.

Three errors, and the third is the acute one. To speak without observing his expression — blindness — is speaking without reading the state of the person you are speaking to.

Which is 15.8 with a third term added (losing the person, losing the words — and now: speaking without having looked), and it is 7.8's requirement that the temperature be tested before anything is delivered.

The observation is squarely within the reconstruction. The form is a numbered list with no occasion — and there is a frequency point worth registering: the X者三 construction clusters in this book and is rare elsewhere.

16.7

Confucius said: "The noble person guards against three things. In youth, when the blood and vital spirits are not yet settled, he guards against lust. In his prime, when they are strong, he guards against strife. In old age, when they are declining, he guards against acquisitiveness."

孔子曰：「君子有三戒：少之时，血气未定，戒之在色；及其壮也，血气方刚，戒之在斗；及其老也，血气既衰，戒之在得。」

Non-anchor.

The observation is good — and the third item is unusually honest: old age's characteristic fault is grasping, which is not the sort of thing a tradition says about its elders.

Two things prevent it anchoring. The numbered form, and — more substantially — the causal frame. Moral tendency is derived from 血气, the physical constitution at each stage. The anchoring material runs the other way: ren proceeds from oneself (12.1); I want ren, and ren is here (7.29); you have drawn a line (6.12).

Not opposed — the observation stands, and one may perfectly well notice that appetites change with age. But a physiology of virtue is a different structure from what the rest of this reading finds.

16.8

Confucius said: "The noble person stands in awe of three things: he stands in awe of what is decreed, in awe of great men, in awe of the words of sages. The small man does not know what is decreed and so is not in awe of it; he is familiar with great men; he makes light of the words of sages."

孔子曰：「君子有三畏：畏天命，畏大人，畏圣人之言。小人不知天命而不畏也，狎大人，侮圣人之言。」

Split judgment: in awe of what is decreed anchors (weak); the other two items do not anchor, and one reading of them is opposed.

Why judge item by item. The three are not of equal grade — one fits what this reading has reconstructed; two require sorting. Judging the chapter whole in either direction would either endorse or discard all three.

Item one: in awe of what is decreed — anchor (weak).

Knowing what is decreed is knowing the boundary between what is in one's hands and what is not — whether the way prevails is fate (14.36); death and life are allotted (12.5); I do not complain against Heaven (14.35). Awe here is the seriousness proper to that boundary, of a piece with respect the spirits and keep them at a distance (6.22) and not yet knowing life, how should one know death (11.12).

Weak because it is an item in a list, with no occasion.

Items two and three: in awe of great men, in awe of the words of sages — non-anchor, and one use is opposed.

What we oppose: the use in which 畏 is construed as may not be questioned, exempting a great man's position or a sage's words from having to give reasons.

Other constructions we do not dispute. 畏 can be read as respect for weight, or seriousness before what is greater — and the reading of 大人 as men of virtue and standing rather than whoever holds power is available. What is opposed is a use, not every possible sense.

Why we oppose it. He told his students not to yield to their teacher on a matter of ren (15.36). He complained that his best student never argued (11.4). He conceded publicly when corrected by a student (17.4) and treated being caught in a fault as good fortune (7.30). He said the minister's duty is to oppose his ruler to his face (14.22). And he named the condition where no one dares contradict as what ruins a state (13.15).

When awe is construed as not to be questioned, and a sage's words are exempted from having to give reasons, the questioner's position is compressed — and the two-way street closes. The two-way street was the method.

And a limit on our own claim, stated plainly: we have no reception-history material establishing that this chapter in fact produced that effect. What is named here is the use this book warns against, not a documented history.

16.9

Confucius said: "Those who are born knowing are the highest; those who learn and so know come next; those who learn under difficulty come next again. Those who face difficulty and do not learn — such are the lowest of the people."

孔子曰：「生而知之者上也，学而知之者次也，困而学之又其次也；困而不学，民斯为下矣。」

Non-anchor, and a reading is opposed.

Non-anchor because it is a four-tier list of knowledge-acquisition, without occasion, addressee or anything at stake — and that alone would place it here.

What we oppose: the reading that takes this as establishing an innate hierarchy of persons, and grades people by it.

What we do not oppose, and this must be said first. We do not object to describing how knowledge is come by. Born knowing, learning readily, learning with difficulty — as a description of routes, this ranks nothing about the people taking them.

What cannot follow is the conversion of that description into a fixed rank in what a person is — with a lower standing as an end, or a warrant to stop teaching.

Why it matters here. Once born knowing is a position someone can occupy, hierarchy has a vocabulary. And the last clause supplies the bottom of it: 民斯为下矣 — such are the lowest.

Two readings of that clause, and this book does not settle between them. Those who face difficulty and do not learn are the lowest sort — a judgment about a disposition. Or: the common people are the lowest — a judgment about a class. If the second, it runs directly into 15.39 (in teaching, no categories) and 2.20's teach those who lack ability.

A correction of our own, recorded here because it was our error. An earlier version of this work argued that 16.9 cannot stand with 7.19 (I am not one who was born knowing). That inference does not hold. A man may decline to place himself in the highest category without denying that the category exists. The argument is withdrawn. What 7.19 does establish is narrower and still useful: he removed himself from the top of this ladder.

And what is opposed is the ranking reading — not a claim about who said the line. This book makes no finding about that.

16.10

Confucius said: "The noble person has nine considerations: in seeing, to see clearly; in hearing, to hear distinctly; in expression, to be warm; in bearing, to be respectful; in speech, to be faithful; in service, to be reverent; in doubt, to ask; in anger, to think of the consequences; in sight of gain, to think of what is right."

孔子曰：「君子有九思：视思明，听思聪，色思温，貌思恭，言思忠，事思敬，疑思问，忿思难，见得思义。」

Non-anchor.

Every one of the nine is defensible, and three of them are the anchoring material in miniature: in doubt, to ask (2.18, 15.26); in anger, to think of the consequences (12.21); in sight of gain, to think of what is right (14.12, 19.1).

Why it does not anchor: there is no addressee and no situation — and a summary in list form cannot serve as evidence of a teaching situation, which is what this reconstruction needs. (Compare 12.1, which has Yan Hui in it; 11.22, which has two men and a bystander.)

And on direction: this book has no material showing that anyone was ever required to treat these nine as an exhaustive, unrevisable compliance schedule. So no finding of that kind is made.

An earlier version of this work argued that the nine items fill in what the four "do nots" of 12.1 left open, and that the completeness of the list is itself the fault. That argument is withdrawn. A list of "things to bear in mind" can remind, can scaffold a beginner, and can be argued with item by item. It acquires a direction only when it is taken as exhaustive and beyond question — and whether that happened is not something this book can show.

The comparison with 12.1 is therefore recorded as a difference of form, and no more: one marks a boundary in negative terms, the other names positive considerations. Neither form settles what was done with it.

16.11

Confucius said: "'Seeing what is good, pursue it as though you could not reach it; seeing what is not good, recoil as from scalding water.' I have seen such men and heard such words. 'Living in retirement to attain one's purpose; practising what is right to carry through one's way.' I have heard such words. I have not seen such a man."

孔子曰：「见善如不及，见不善如探汤。吾见其人矣，吾闻其语矣。隐居以求其志，行义以达其道。吾闻其语矣，未见其人也。」

Non-anchor.

The distinction is real and precise. The first pair is keenness of response — and he has met such people. The second is the joining of withdrawal and action — holding one's purpose in retirement and carrying it through when in the world (with 7.10's employed, act; set aside, withdraw). And he has not met anyone doing it.

The calibration is his — I have heard the words; I have not seen the man (4.6, 5.27). The form is a quoted-saying commentary.

16.12

Duke Jing of Qi had a thousand teams of horses; on the day he died, the people found no virtue to praise him for. Bo Yi and Shu Qi starved at the foot of Shouyang, and the people praise them to this day.

齐景公有马千驷，死之日，民无德而称焉。伯夷、叔齐饿于首阳之下，民到于今称之。

Non-anchor, and the text is defective (some editions carry a fragment of a preceding quotation).

The contrast is the standard one — what people remember is not wealth (with 14.33, 15.20).

16.13

Chen Kang asked Boyu: "Have you heard anything different from the rest of us?" He replied: "No. He was once standing alone, and I hurried across the courtyard. He said: 'Have you studied the Odes?' I said: 'Not yet.' 'If you do not study the Odes, you will have nothing to say.' I withdrew and studied the Odes. Another day he was standing alone again, and I hurried across the courtyard. He said: 'Have you studied ritual?' 'Not yet.' 'If you do not study ritual, you will have no footing.' I withdrew and studied ritual. I have heard these two things." Chen Kang withdrew and said, delighted: "I asked one thing and got three. I heard about the Odes, I heard about ritual, and I heard that the noble person keeps his son at a distance."

陈亢问于伯鱼曰：「子亦有异闻乎？」……陈亢退而喜曰：「问一得三：闻《诗》，闻礼，又闻君子之远其子也。」

Anchor (core). The one anchor in this book, and it is decisive.

A student suspects there is an inner teaching, and goes to check with the teacher's own son.

And the answer is: nothing. Two exchanges in a courtyard, both of them things every student in the school had heard — study the Odes, study ritual. No private curriculum.

With 7.23 (do you take me to be keeping something from you? I keep nothing from you) and 15.3 (the one thread, left unexplained to Zigong as well). Three independent passages, and no hidden doctrine turns up in any of them.

And Chen Kang's own summary is the best line in the book: I asked one thing and got three. He went looking for esoterica and came back with the fact that there was none — and counted it as a gain.

16.14

The wife of a ruler is called by the ruler "lady"; she calls herself "little child"; the people of the state call her "the ruler's lady"; before other states she is called "the ruler's little lady"; and people of other states call her "the ruler's lady."

邦君之妻，君称之曰夫人，夫人自称曰小童；邦人称之曰君夫人，称诸异邦曰寡小君；异邦人称之亦曰君夫人。

Non-anchor.

A page of ritual protocol on forms of address. No the Master said, no addressee, no doctrine — nothing that connects it to the rest of the book. Most likely a fragment of a ritual manual, or an editor's appendix.

One thing in its favour, worth recording: it does not borrow his voice. It does not say the Master said. Whoever placed it here added material without attributing it — which is more than can be said for some of what surrounds it.

Book 16 in sum. One core anchor, and it is 16.13 — the chapter establishing that there was no private teaching.

Everything else is the compiled layer: six numbered sets, two pieces of historical commentary, one long assembled speech, a page of protocol.

And the important thing is how little of it is opposed. Two chapters carry a stated opposition (16.8's second and third items; 16.9's ranking reading) and one clause is flagged (16.2's the common people do not discuss it). The rest — the friendships, the pleasures, the errors in attendance, the three cautions, the nine considerations — is not wrong. It has no one in it.

Which is the difference this whole volume turns on: this cannot carry the reconstruction is not this is false.

Book 17 · 阳货 Yang Huo

The most mixed book in the Analects, and the one where the stakes are highest. It contains the strongest statement in the text that people start close together (17.2) — and, one chapter later, the line most often used to undo it (17.3). It contains the passage in which he is corrected by a student in public and concedes on the spot (17.4), his own account of why he wanted to stop speaking (17.19), the moment he hands a judgment back to a student who disagreed with him (17.21) — and the sentence this book opposes most strongly of anything in the Analects (17.25).

17.1

Yang Huo wanted Confucius to call on him, and Confucius would not go. He sent Confucius a suckling pig. Confucius chose a time when he was out to go and offer thanks, and met him on the road. He said to Confucius: "Come, let me speak with you. Can one be called ren who keeps his treasure in his breast and lets his state go astray? — I would say not. Can one be called wise who is eager to take part in affairs and repeatedly misses the moment? — I would say not. The days and months are passing; the years do not wait for us." Confucius said: "Very well. I shall take office."

阳货欲见孔子，孔子不见，归孔子豚。孔子时其亡也而往拜之，遇诸涂……孔子曰：「诺，吾将仕矣。」

Anchor (core).

Yang Huo had seized power in Lu as a retainer of the Ji family — a usurper's usurper. He wants Confucius in his government, and uses a rule of ritual to force a visit: a gift from a superior must be acknowledged in person.

And the manoeuvring in reply is exact. He does not refuse the courtesy — he goes when the man is out. He does not refuse the argument — he agrees with it in general terms. I shall take office — with no when, no under whom.

Nothing is conceded and nothing is broken. This is 9.30's weighing in operation — the ritual is observed, the recruitment is declined, and neither is made into an occasion for a stand.

17.2

"By nature, close together; by practice, far apart."

子曰：「性相近也，习相远也。」

Anchor (core). One of the two or three most consequential lines in the book.

And it must be read exactly, because both over-reading and under-reading it cause trouble.

"Close" is not "identical." This reading takes the line as: people are close in what makes them people, and that commonality precedes any ranking derived from their differences.

The second clause — by practice, far apart — says that practice shapes and widens difference. It does not carry a universal claim that all difference originates after birth, and this book does not assert one.

Read this way, it is the ground of in teaching, no categories (15.39) — and the ground does not require that people be equally endowed. Teaching is open to all not because everyone starts level, but because a difference is not a reason for exclusion.

And that is the form in which the claim is defensible. A claim that all people are identically capable would be false and would not be needed. What is needed is that no difference converts into a lower standing as an end.

17.3

"Only the highest wisdom and the lowest folly do not shift."

子曰：「唯上知与下愚不移。」

Set aside as contested — and a reading is opposed.

The text's status. The sense cannot be settled from the text. 不移 can be read as cannot be changed — a claim about innate fixity — or as do not shift — a description of extreme stability. This book does not claim the line uniquely establishes a doctrine of innate castes.

What we oppose: the use of this chapter as the master gloss on 17.2, taken to establish that some people are unteachable by birth, and licensing the abandonment of teaching for them.

Why. That reading narrows 17.2's openness at exactly the point that matters. By nature close together says the starting places are near one another and that practice does the separating; if do not shift is read as innate fixity, the two ends are frozen — the highest wise and the lowest foolish, each in place before anything happens.

And there is a class of person it is useless to teach is precisely what in teaching, no categories (15.39) excludes. It is also excluded by teach those who lack ability (2.20), by the calf of the plough-ox (6.6), by I approve his coming forward; I do not vouch for his past (7.28), and by from anyone who has brought a bundle of dried meat (7.7).

The mechanism is worth naming, because it recurs. Nothing in 17.2 is touched. An exception is placed beside it, and later readers use the exception to interpret the rule — so that by nature close together comes to mean close together, apart from the two ends.

And what we do not conclude: we make no finding about where this line came from, and none about who is responsible for the use made of it. What is opposed is a reading.

17.4

The Master went to Wucheng and heard the sound of stringed instruments and singing. He smiled and said: "Why use an ox-cleaver to kill a chicken?" Ziyou replied: "Formerly I heard you say, Master: 'When the noble person has learned the way, he loves people; when the small man has learned the way, he is easy to employ.'" The Master said: "My young men, Yan's words are right. What I said before was a joke."

子之武城，闻弦歌之声。夫子莞尔而笑曰：「割鸡焉用牛刀？」子游对曰：「昔者偃也闻诸夫子曰：『君子学道则爱人，小人学道则易使也。』」子曰：「二三子！偃之言是也，前言戏之耳。」

Anchor (core). The teacher corrected in public, conceding in public.

He teases his student for using ritual and music in a small town. And Ziyou quotes his own teaching back at him.

And the concession is immediate and addressed to everyone present: My young men — Yan's words are right. What I said before was a joke.

Three things. He does not defend the joke. He does not concede privately — he turns to the room, so that the students who heard the jibe hear the retraction. And he names the student as correct.

With 6.2 (Yong's words are right), 3.8 (it is Shang who draws me out), 5.9 — but this is the sharpest of the four, because here he was wrong and the student was right, in front of witnesses.

And note what Ziyou's quotation contains: when the small man has learned the way, he is easy to employ — which is the teaching of common people, in a small town, being carried out. His own principle, being applied further than he had thought to apply it.

17.5

Gongshan Furao held Bi in revolt and summoned him, and the Master wanted to go. Zilu was displeased and said: "There may be nowhere to go, but why must it be to Gongshan?" The Master said: "The man who summons me — would he do so for nothing? If anyone were to use me, might I not make a Zhou in the east?"

公山弗扰以费畔，召，子欲往。子路不说，曰：「末之也已，何必公山氏之之也？」子曰：「夫召我者，而岂徒哉？如有用我者，吾其为东周乎！」

Non-anchor.

Preserved, and it does the school no credit — he wanted to accept an invitation from a man in armed revolt, and was talked out of it by a student.

What it records is real and unflattering: the desire to be used was strong enough to be tempted by this. With 9.13 (sell it! sell it!) and 17.7 below.

Non-anchoring because there is no judgment being demonstrated here — an impulse and a rebuke, without resolution. But its preservation is worth noting: a school protecting its founder does not keep this.

17.6

Zizhang asked Confucius about ren. Confucius said: "One who can practise five things everywhere under heaven has ren." "May I ask what they are?" "Respectfulness, tolerance, good faith, diligence, generosity. Being respectful, you are not insulted; being tolerant, you win the multitude; keeping good faith, you are trusted with responsibility; being diligent, you achieve results; being generous, you can command others."

子张问仁于孔子。孔子曰：「能行五者于天下为仁矣。」……「恭、宽、信、敏、惠。恭则不侮，宽则得众，信则人任焉，敏则有功，惠则足以使人。」

Non-anchor, and the reason is instructive.

Five items with five yields attached — respectfulness yields freedom from insult, tolerance yields a following, generosity yields the ability to command.

And the direction has quietly turned. Every one of the five is paid for by what it gets you. Compare the answers to Yan Hui, Zhonggong and Sima Niu (12.1–12.3): none of them has a return attached. Compare 6.22: put the difficulty first and the gain after.

Here the gain is the argument. Being generous, you can command others — generosity as a technique of management.

Not opposed — nothing in the five is bad, and one may perfectly well notice that decent conduct has good effects. What cannot anchor is a version of ren whose case is made from the returns.

17.7

Bi Xi summoned him, and the Master wanted to go. Zilu said: "Formerly I heard you say, Master: 'A noble person does not enter where a man is doing evil in his own person.' Bi Xi is holding Zhongmou in revolt. How can you go?" The Master said: "Yes, I did say that. But is it not said: so hard, that grinding does not wear it thin? Is it not said: so white, that dyeing does not turn it black? Am I a bitter gourd, to be hung up and not eaten?"

佛肸召，子欲往。子路曰：「昔者由也闻诸夫子曰：『亲于其身为不善者，君子不入也。』佛肸以中牟畔，子之往也，如之何？」子曰：「然，有是言也。……吾岂匏瓜也哉？焉能系而不食？」

Non-anchor.

The same situation as 17.5, and this time he answers the objection — and the answer is a rationalization. Grinding does not wear the hard thin; dyeing does not turn the white black.

And then the honest part: am I a bitter gourd, to be hung up and not eaten? He wants to be used, and says so.

Twice in one book, and Zilu stops him both times. The record preserves the temptation, the excuse, and the student who was right.

17.8

The Master said: "You! Have you heard of the six sayings and the six obscurings?" He replied: "I have not." "Sit down, and I will tell you. To love ren without loving learning — the obscuring is foolishness. To love knowing without loving learning — the obscuring is dissipation. To love good faith without loving learning — the obscuring is harm. To love straightness without loving learning — the obscuring is cutting. To love courage without loving learning — the obscuring is disorder. To love firmness without loving learning — the obscuring is recklessness."

子曰：「由也！女闻六言六蔽矣乎？」……「好仁不好学，其蔽也愚；好知不好学，其蔽也荡；好信不好学，其蔽也贼；好直不好学，其蔽也绞；好勇不好学，其蔽也乱；好刚不好学，其蔽也狂。」

Non-anchor.

The content is exactly right and the form is not his. Six virtues, six failures — and the structural point is genuine: each virtue, uninformed by learning, runs to its own characteristic ruin. (Compare 8.2, where four qualities without ritual go wrong in four directions.)

Six parallel clauses, addressed by name and delivered as a set — the X者六 mould of Book 16, with Zilu inserted as the recipient.

(And note: 8.2's version does the same work with four items and no template, and anchors.)

17.9

"My young men, why do none of you study the Odes? The Odes can stir you; they can be used for observing; they can make you fit for company; they can express grievance. Near at hand, they teach you to serve your father; further off, to serve your ruler. And you learn the names of many birds, beasts, plants and trees."

子曰：「小子何莫学夫诗？诗，可以兴，可以观，可以群，可以怨。迩之事父，远之事君。多识于鸟兽草木之名。」

Anchor (core).

Four uses, and the fourth is the one that could not have been invented by a school: 怨 — the Odes can express grievance.

A canon assembled for moral instruction does not list complaining among the reasons to study it. And it sits with 3.20 (grief without injury) and 2.2 (thought without swerve): feeling running its full course, and not being smoothed.

And the last clause is unguarded and charming — you learn the names of many birds, beasts, plants and trees. No moral is attached to it.

17.10

The Master said to Boyu: "Have you worked at the Zhou Nan and the Shao Nan? A man who has not is like one standing with his face to a wall."

子谓伯鱼曰：「女为《周南》、《召南》矣乎？人而不为《周南》、《召南》，其犹正墙面而立也与？」

Anchor (probable).

An image, and the closing particles leave it as a question (with 16.13's courtyard exchanges — the same content, and the same complete absence of anything special for a son).

17.11

"Ritual, they say, ritual! Is it jade and silk that is meant? Music, they say, music! Is it bells and drums that is meant?"

子曰：「礼云礼云，玉帛云乎哉？乐云乐云，钟鼓云乎哉？」

Anchor (core).

Two questions, and nothing is asserted. The jade and silk are the instruments of ritual; the bells and drums the instruments of music. Neither question says what ritual is.

Which is the method: the answer — that ritual is ren in outward operation (3.3) — is not supplied here. The question empties the vessel and leaves it empty.

17.12

"To be stern in appearance and weak within — to take a comparison from the small man, is it not like being a thief who bores through walls?"

子曰：「色厉而内荏，譬诸小人，其犹穿窬之盗也与？」

Anchor (core).

The comparison is precise rather than merely abusive. A man who is stern outside and hollow inside is taking authority he has not got — which is theft, and the image is of the burglar who goes in through the wall rather than the door.

With 12.20's man of renown who assumes the appearance of ren and acts otherwise, and 17.13 below.

17.13

"The village worthy is the thief of virtue."

子曰：「乡愿，德之贼也。」

Anchor (core).

The 乡愿 is the man everyone in the village speaks well of — obliging, inoffensive, never on any side.

And he is called the thief of virtue, which is harsher than anything said about an outright bad man.

Why. Because he demonstrates that virtue is a matter of giving no offence — and once that is the model, the thing itself has been replaced by its appearance, with everyone's approval. An obvious villain teaches nobody that villainy is virtue. This man teaches everyone that inoffensiveness is.

With 13.24 (not enough that all his neighbours like him) and 15.28 (when everyone likes a man, you must examine it). The three chapters are one argument, and this is its sharpest form.

17.14

"To hear something on the road and repeat it on the road is to throw virtue away."

子曰：「道听而涂说，德之弃也。」

Anchor (core).

The fault is the interval — there is none. Heard and passed on, with nothing in between.

Which is the opposite of the "leave a gap" discipline (2.18: hear much, set aside what is doubtful, speak the rest with care; 15.26: the scribe who left blanks). What is thrown away is the pause in which one would have found out whether it is so.

17.15

"Can one serve a ruler alongside a mean man? Before he gets it, he is anxious to get it. Having got it, he is anxious not to lose it. And once he is anxious about losing it, there is nothing he will not do."

子曰：「鄙夫可与事君也与哉？其未得之也，患得之；既得之，患失之。苟患失之，无所不至矣。」

Anchor (core).

A complete causal account in three clauses, and the last is the finding: there is nothing he will not do.

Because the anxiety has no floor. Whoever holds a position as the thing he is has no limit on what he will do to keep it — the limits would have to come from somewhere the position does not reach.

With 5.11 (Cheng has desires — how can he be firm?): whoever wants something can be gripped by it, and what can grip a man can move him anywhere.

17.16

"In old times people had three faults, which perhaps are not to be found now. The wild of old were unconstrained; the wild now are dissolute. The stiff of old had edges; the stiff now are quarrelsome and perverse. The foolish of old were straight; the foolish now are merely deceitful."

子曰：「古者民有三疾，今也或是之亡也。古之狂也肆，今之狂也荡；古之矜也廉，今之矜也忿戾；古之愚也直，今之愚也诈而已矣。」

Anchor (probable).

The structure is unexpected: three faults, and the complaint is that the faults have deteriorated.

Each old version had something in it. Unconstrained wildness had freedom; stiffness had edges; foolishness at least had straightness. The current versions have lost the redeeming part and kept the fault.

Which is a more interesting claim than "things were better before": what is mourned is not the absence of vice but the loss of what was salvageable inside it (with 13.21, where the ardent and the scrupulous are chosen precisely for what their excesses contain).

17.17

"Clever words and an ingratiating face — seldom is there ren in these."

子曰：「巧言令色，鲜矣仁。」

Anchor. Identical to 1.3 — two transmission streams. See 1.3.

17.18

"I hate the way purple takes away from vermilion. I hate the way the tunes of Zheng confuse classical music. I hate the way sharp tongues overthrow states and families."

子曰：「恶紫之夺朱也，恶郑声之乱雅乐也，恶利口之覆邦家者。」

Anchor (core).

Three hatreds, and all three are the same structure: the near-thing displacing the thing.

Purple is a mixed colour close to vermilion. The tunes of Zheng are more pleasing than the classical music. A sharp tongue is more persuasive than a sound argument. In each case what wins is what resembles and is easier.

This is the general form of what 17.13 says about the village worthy — and of what 12.20 says about renown. The counterfeit's advantage is that it is more attractive than the original.

17.19

The Master said: "I wish to say nothing." Zigong said: "If you say nothing, what shall we, your disciples, have to transmit?" The Master said: "What does Heaven say? The four seasons run their course; the hundred things come into being. What does Heaven say?"

子曰：「予欲无言。」子贡曰：「子如不言，则小子何述焉？」子曰：「天何言哉？四时行焉，百物生焉，天何言哉？」

Anchor (core).

And the reading must be got right, because the obvious one is wrong.

I wish to say nothing is not about saying less.

On this book's definition of the remainder — not the content deliberately withheld, but what cannot be completed for another person however fully things are explained — what this chapter says is that the highest thing is not on the speaking side at all.

The four seasons run and the hundred things come to be, and nothing was said. And a student's own arriving does not come about by his teacher's saying it either.

So this is not "he stopped talking." He did not — the book is full of his talk. He could explain ritual, the Odes, government and ren in as much detail as was wanted, and did. What he could not do, by any amount of talking, was the one thing that had to be Zigong's.

And Zigong's question — what shall we have to transmit? — is the whole difficulty in one line. He wants something transmissible. That is precisely what is not available.

(An earlier version of this work read this chapter as "the more one says, the less remainder is left." That reading is withdrawn: it followed from the older definition of the remainder as unsaid content, and it does not survive the definition this work now uses.)

17.20

Ru Bei wanted to see Confucius. Confucius declined on grounds of illness. As the messenger was going out of the door, he took up his lute and sang, so that the man should hear it.

孺悲欲见孔子，孔子辞以疾。将命者出户，取瑟而歌，使之闻之。

Anchor (core).

He refuses, and then makes sure the refusal is known to be a refusal.

The illness was a formula — a polite fiction everyone understood. And he takes up the lute so that the messenger hears it and reports it.

Which is exactly not deception. The convention allowed him to decline without saying so; he declined and then declined to hide it. The man is refused, and is not lied to.

With 15.1 (the answer about vessels, and leaving the next day) — withdrawal that is legible as withdrawal.

17.21

Zai Wo asked: "Three years of mourning — a year is surely long enough. If the noble person performs no ritual for three years, ritual will decay; if he makes no music for three years, music will collapse. In a year the old grain is finished, the new grain is up, and the fire-drills have been changed. A year is enough." The Master said: "Would you feel at ease eating rice and wearing brocade?" "I would." "If you would feel at ease, then do it. When the noble person is in mourning, fine food gives him no pleasure, music gives him no joy, comfort makes him uneasy — so he does not do it. But if you would feel at ease, then do it." When Zai Wo had gone out, the Master said: "How lacking in ren Yu is! A child does not leave its parents' arms until it is three years old…"

宰我问：「三年之丧，期已久矣……期可已矣。」子曰：「食夫稻，衣夫锦，于女安乎？」曰：「安。」「女安则为之！……」宰我出，子曰：「予之不仁也！……」

Anchor (core). And it is the clearest case in the book of a judgment handed back.

Zai Wo makes an argument, and it is a good argument — three years of not practising will damage the practices themselves, and the natural year has completed its cycle.

And the reply is not a counter-argument. It is a question about him: would you feel at ease?

He says he would. And he is told: then do it.

Twice. If you would feel at ease, then do it.

What is not done here matters more than what is. He does not overrule him. He does not produce the authority he obviously has. He puts the question at the only place it can be answered — inside Zai Wo — and abides by the answer.

And then, when the man has left, he says to the room that Zai Wo is without ren.

Both halves are necessary and neither cancels the other. He would not compel the observance — that would have been to complete the judgment on Zai Wo's behalf. And he did not pretend the outcome was fine.

Cultivation is not a guarantee of results. It is a refusal to obtain results by the other means.

17.22

"To eat one's fill all day and never use one's mind on anything — that is a hard case. Are there not games with dice and chess? Even to play those would be better than nothing."

子曰：「饱食终日，无所用心，难矣哉！不有博弈者乎？为之犹贤乎已。」

Anchor (core).

And the second half is the interesting one. Having named the condition, he offers a floor: board games. Better than nothing.

Which is the same move as 14.12's second answer (what need for a complete person to be like that?): when the standard cannot be met, the standard is lowered rather than the person written off.

17.23

Zilu said: "Does the noble person esteem courage?" The Master said: "The noble person puts what is right above all. A noble person with courage and without what is right will make disorder. A small man with courage and without what is right will become a thief."

子路曰：「君子尚勇乎？」子曰：「君子义以为上。君子有勇而无义为乱，小人有勇而无义为盗。」

Anchor (core).

Asked by the bravest man in the school whether courage is esteemed, and the answer is: yi is above it.

Courage is not a virtue in itself but a multiplier — and the two consequences named scale with position: a noble person's courage without yi produces disorder in a state; a small man's produces theft.

With 8.2 (courage without ritual becomes disorder) and 15.18 (yi as the substance, the rest as its operations).

17.24

Zigong said: "Does the noble person also have hatreds?" The Master said: "He has. He hates those who proclaim other people's badness. He hates those in low position who slander their superiors. He hates the courageous who lack ritual. He hates the resolute who are obstinate and closed." He said: "And you, Ci — have you hatreds?" "I hate those who take plagiarism for knowledge. I hate the unhumble who take it for courage. I hate those who expose others and take it for straightness."

子贡曰：「君子亦有恶乎？」子曰：「有恶：恶称人之恶者，恶居下流而讪上者，恶勇而无礼者，恶果敢而窒者。」曰：「赐也亦有恶乎？」「恶徼以为知者，恶不孙以为勇者，恶讦以为直者。」

Anchor (core).

Hatred is admitted — and 4.3 said as much: only one with ren is able to like people and able to dislike them.

And what is hated in every one of the seven items is the same thing: something being passed off as something else. Proclaiming faults passed off as honesty; obstinacy as resolution; and in Zigong's three, all explicitly: plagiarism taken for knowledge, rudeness for courage, exposure for straightness.

And he asks the student for his list — and Zigong's is as good as his own.

17.25

"Only women and small men are hard to keep. Bring them close, and they are not humble; keep them at a distance, and they resent it."

子曰：「唯女子与小人为难养也，近之则不孙，远之则怨。」

Set aside as contested — and this book's strongest single opposition.

Four things stated separately, because they are four different questions.

Text: set aside. The sense is disputed. 小人 may carry its ordinary sense in this book — one who has not yet become a noble person, a position rather than a class. 难养 may describe difficulty in an unequal household relation rather than a claim about natures. And this book has no variant edition to point to.

Attribution: not judged. We make no finding about who said this, or when it entered the text.

What is opposed: any reading that builds from this line a claim that women are by nature lesser, unteachable, or generally difficult to deal with.

Force: strong — and it does not weaken because the text is set aside.

Why.

The ground is structural, not a matter of finding pleasanter sayings elsewhere.

17.2 puts people close together in what makes them people, and locates difference in practice. A negative claim about half of humanity, fixed by birth, has no place to stand in that.

15.39 — in teaching, no categories — makes no class of person a condition of admission. If there were a kind of person who is constitutionally hard to deal with and cannot be brought on, "no categories" is already false.

And 恕 — the one word to live by (15.24) — runs only if the other party is a subject as you are. What you do not want done to you, do not do to others presupposes that their not-wanting counts as yours does. Divide that, and the inference fails. This is the sharpest of the three: the sentence he named as the single word for a lifetime does not survive the division.

Add the pattern of his judgments — Guan Zhong assessed on what he preserved, not on his loyalty (14.16–17); the three worthies of Yin assessed on three different courses (18.1); the calf of the plough-ox judged on itself and not its parentage (6.6). Nowhere else is a different scale used for a category of person.

A textual observation, with its limit stated.

Throughout the Analects, 女 in his mouth functions as 汝, "you" — 17.8 (女闻六言六蔽矣乎), 17.10, 17.21 (于女安乎), 2.17, 5.4, 6.13, 15.3. The compound 女子 in the sense of "woman" occurs here alone.

And this is an anomaly and nothing more. A compound is not decided by the ordinary sense of its first character. We have no variant text supporting any other reading. The observation is recorded because it is a fact about the book's own usage; it is not evidence for a conclusion, and no conclusion is drawn from it.

(A possible reading, offered and not asserted: without those three characters the line would read "only small men are hard to keep; bring them close and they are not humble, keep them at a distance and they resent it" — a standard junzi/xiaoren observation, and one that pairs directly with 13.25's the small man is hard to serve and easy to please. This is a possibility this book raises. It is not a finding.)

And a question for the reader.

Anyone building a case for the inferiority of women out of this line must explain why they take a sentence whose sense is disputed and whose usage is unique in the book, in preference to two plain general statements — in teaching, no categories, and by nature close together.

Choosing the one over the others is the reader's act, not the text's.

17.26

"A man who at forty is disliked has come to his end."

子曰：「年四十而见恶焉，其终也已。」

Non-anchor.

And the comparison with 9.23 is the reason. There: a man not heard of by forty or fifty is not, after all, worth being in awe of — the awe is withdrawn, not the person. Here: 其终也已 — that is the end of him. A door closed.

Which does not sit with I do not vouch for his past (7.28), with the shoots that may still flower (9.22), or with a method built on people not being finished.

Non-anchoring rather than opposed — the sense is uncertain and no structure is being erected on it.

Book 17 in sum. Fifteen core anchors, and the widest range in the Analects.

Two of them are the reason this book can be written at all: 17.4, where he is corrected in public and concedes to the room; and 17.21, where he hands a judgment back to a student who disagreed with him, abides by the answer, and does not pretend to be satisfied with it.

And two of the four things this book opposes are here. 17.3, which — read as the gloss on 17.2 — freezes the two ends of humanity in place. And 17.25, against which this book states its strongest objection, on the ground that the sentence he named as the one word to live by does not survive the division it makes.

Book 18 · 微子 Wei Zi

The book of the men who withdrew — and of his answers to them.

Three encounters with recluses (18.5, 18.6, 18.7), each of which tells him to his face that the world cannot be helped and he is a fool for trying. The school kept all three.

And it opens with the chapter this reading takes as the clearest single statement of how it judges (18.1): three men, three completely different courses, and all three called ren.

18.1

The Viscount of Wei left him; the Viscount of Ji became his slave; Bi Gan remonstrated and died. Confucius said: "The Yin had three men of ren."

微子去之，箕子为之奴，比干谏而死。孔子曰：「殷有三仁焉。」

Anchor (core).

Three men under the same tyrant, and three incompatible responses. One left. One stayed and survived by feigning madness, and was enslaved. One spoke, and was killed for it.

And all three are called ren.

Which settles what the word is measuring. Not the course of action — the three courses exclude one another; any rule that endorsed one would condemn the other two. What is being assessed is whether, in the position each man was in, he acted with the people as the end — and each did, differently.

This is the same judgment as Guan Zhong's (14.16–17), and the two together fix the criterion: the question is never did he follow the rule, and always what happened to people, and what was he doing about it from where he stood.

18.2

Liuxia Hui, as chief judge, was dismissed three times. Someone said: "Is it not time for you to leave?" He said: "If I serve people by the straight way, where shall I go and not be dismissed three times? If I serve people by a crooked way, what need is there to leave the land of my parents?"

柳下惠为士师，三黜。人曰：「子未可以去乎？」曰：「直道而事人，焉往而不三黜？枉道而事人，何必去父母之邦？」

Anchor (core) — though the words are not his.

Two questions, and between them they exhaust the alternatives. Serve straight, and dismissal follows anywhere — so leaving solves nothing. Serve crooked, and there is no reason to go anywhere — you could do that at home.

Which reframes the question he was asked. Should I leave? is not the real question. The real one is which way one intends to serve — and once that is settled, the geography does not matter.

This anchors although Liuxia Hui was not his student. (With 19.3, 19.19, 12.9 — what matters is whether the passage carries the structure.)

18.3

Duke Jing of Qi, considering how to receive Confucius, said: "I cannot treat him as the Ji family are treated. I will receive him at a level between the Ji and the Meng." Then he said: "I am old; I cannot use him." And Confucius left.

齐景公待孔子曰：「若季氏，则吾不能；以季孟之间待之。」曰：「吾老矣，不能用也。」孔子行。

Anchor (probable).

A negotiation about rank, followed by a refusal, followed by departure.

And what is recorded is that he left when the answer came — no lingering, no attempt to improve the terms. (With 15.1, 18.4.)

18.4

The people of Qi sent a present of women musicians. Ji Huanzi accepted them, and for three days no court was held. Confucius left.

齐人归女乐，季桓子受之，三日不朝，孔子行。

Anchor (probable).

The reason for leaving is stated by the sequence and not by any comment. Not that he was unemployed — he was employed. The man in power stopped governing for three days.

And there is no reproach in the text. The record is: this happened, and he left.

The three recluses (18.5–18.7)

18.5

Jieyu, the madman of Chu, passed Confucius, singing: "Phoenix! Phoenix! How your virtue has declined! What is past cannot be remonstrated with; what is to come can still be pursued. Give it up! Give it up! Those in government now are in danger." Confucius got down, wishing to speak with him. But he hurried away and avoided him, and Confucius could not speak with him.

楚狂接舆歌而过孔子曰：「凤兮凤兮！何德之衰？往者不可谏，来者犹可追。已而！已而！今之从政者殆而！」孔子下，欲与之言。趋而辟之，不得与之言。

Anchor (core).

A stranger sings a warning at him, and he gets down from the carriage to talk.

And the recluse runs away.

What is recorded is the getting down. He is being mocked as a phoenix in decline and his response is to want the conversation — with someone who has just told him to give up.

And he does not get it. The chapter ends with him standing in the road, having failed to speak with a man who would not stay.

18.6

Changju and Jieni were ploughing together. Confucius, passing by, sent Zilu to ask them where the ford was. Changju said: "Who is that holding the reins?" Zilu said: "It is Kong Qiu." "The Kong Qiu of Lu?" "Yes." "Then he knows the ford." Zilu asked Jieni. Jieni said: "Who are you?" "I am Zhong You." "The disciple of Kong Qiu of Lu?" "Yes." "The whole world is a flood — who is going to change it? Rather than follow a man who withdraws from this person and that person, would you not do better to follow those who withdraw from the age altogether?" And he went on covering the seed. Zilu went and reported. The Master was disconcerted, and said: "One cannot flock with the birds and beasts. If I am not to associate with these people, with whom shall I associate? If the way prevailed in the world, I should not be trying to change it."

长沮、桀溺耦而耕……夫子怃然曰：「鸟兽不可与同群！吾非斯人之徒与而谁与？天下有道，丘不与易也。」

Anchor (core). His fullest answer to the case for withdrawal, and it is three sentences.

The recluse's argument is good. The flood is universal; changing it is beyond anyone; and a man who selects which rulers to serve is doing something less coherent than a man who has given up on all of them.

And 怃然 is recorded — he was disconcerted. He did not have an answer ready.

Then:

One cannot flock with the birds and beasts. The alternative to human society is not another society.

If I am not to associate with these people, with whom shall I associate? 这些人 — these people, the ones actually here, the flood included. There is no other set of people available.

If the way prevailed in the world, I should not be trying to change it. — and this is the sentence. The recluse's argument is that the world is too bad to be worth the effort. The answer is that its being bad is the reason for the effort. The premise is accepted entirely, and the conclusion is refused.

With 14.38 — the one who knows it cannot be done and does it anyway. This chapter is that description, argued out.

18.7

Zilu, following, fell behind, and met an old man carrying a basket on a staff. Zilu asked: "Have you seen my master?" The old man said: "Your four limbs do not toil; you cannot tell the five grains apart. Who is your master?" And he planted his staff and began weeding. Zilu stood with his hands joined in respect. The old man kept him for the night, killed a chicken and prepared millet, and presented his two sons to him. Next day Zilu went on and reported. The Master said: "He is a recluse," and sent Zilu back to see him. When he arrived, the man had gone. Zilu said: "Not to take office is not right. If the proper relations of old and young cannot be set aside, how can the duty of ruler and subject be set aside? Wishing to keep himself unsullied, he throws the great relation into confusion. The noble person takes office and does what is right. That the way does not prevail, he knows already."

子路从而后，遇丈人，以杖荷蓧……子路曰：「不仕无义。长幼之节，不可废也；君臣之义，如之何其废之？欲洁其身，而乱大伦。君子之仕也，行其义也。道之不行，已知之矣。」

Anchor (core).

And the argument is made against the recluse out of the recluse's own conduct. He presented his two sons to Zilu — that is, he observed the relation of old and young. He cannot then hold that the relation of ruler and subject may be discarded; he has not discarded the other.

And the closing clause is the sharpest thing a student says in the Analects: That the way does not prevail, he knows already.

Which concedes the recluse's entire factual claim and removes its force. We are not going out because we expect to succeed.

18.8

Men who withdrew: Bo Yi, Shu Qi, Yu Zhong, Yi Yi, Zhu Zhang, Liuxia Hui, Shao Lian. The Master said: "Not lowering their purpose, not disgracing themselves — that was Bo Yi and Shu Qi." Of Liuxia Hui and Shao Lian: "They lowered their purpose and disgraced themselves; but their words were in accord with propriety and their actions with what people had thought out. That is all." Of Yu Zhong and Yi Yi: "They lived in seclusion and were free in what they said. Their persons remained pure, and their withdrawal was suited to the times. But I am different from all of these: I have no preconception of what may and may not be done."

逸民：伯夷、叔齐、虞仲、夷逸、朱张、柳下惠、少连……「我则异于是，无可无不可。」

Anchor (core). And the last clause is the point of the whole book.

Seven men are assessed, in three groups, and each group is credited with something real. No one is dismissed.

And then: I am different from all of these — 无可无不可.

No preconception of what may and may not be done. Nothing settled in advance about whether one serves or withdraws.

Which is 4.10 in the first person (no predisposition for and none against; he goes with what is right) and 9.30's weighing at the top of the ladder.

And note what it makes of the three recluse encounters just before it. He is not the opposite of a recluse. He is the man who has not decided in advance.

18.9

The Grand Music-master Zhi went to Qi. Gan, musician of the second course, went to Chu; Liao of the third to Cai; Que of the fourth to Qin. Fang Shu the drummer went to the Yellow River; Wu of the hand-drum went to the Han; Yang the assistant master and Xiang of the stone chimes went to the sea.

大师挚适齐，亚饭干适楚，三饭缭适蔡，四饭缺适秦，鼓方叔入于河，播鼗武入于汉，少师阳、击磬襄入于海。

Non-anchor.

A roster of departures — the whole musical establishment of a state, scattering in eight directions.

And the effect is achieved entirely by the list. Nothing is said about what it means. The music of a state going out, one man at a time, to Qi and Chu and Cai and Qin and the rivers and the sea.

Non-anchoring: it is a record, with no one being taught. But it is not nothing — 8.15 and 9.15 show what the music mattered to him, and this is what became of it.

18.10

The Duke of Zhou said to the Duke of Lu: "A noble person does not neglect his relatives, nor give his great ministers cause to resent not being employed. Old associates, without grave cause, he does not cast off. He does not look for everything in one person."

周公谓鲁公曰：「君子不施其亲，不使大臣怨乎不以。故旧无大故，则不弃也。无求备于一人。」

Non-anchor.

Four injunctions, all of them generous, and the last is excellent — do not look for everything in one person (with 13.25's the small man requires everything of them).

Not opposed, and not his — the words are the Duke of Zhou's, and this book does not treat them as material for reconstructing his teaching.

18.11

Zhou had eight officers: Bo Da, Bo Kuo, Zhong Tu, Zhong Hu, Shu Ye, Shu Xia, Ji Sui, Ji Gua.

周有八士：伯达、伯适、仲突、仲忽、叔夜、叔夏、季随、季騧。

Non-anchor.

Eight names, and nothing else — no deeds, no assessment, no the Master said.

A list, and it is recorded here as one. (With 18.9 — both are rosters of men, one dispersing and one merely named.)

Book 18 in sum. Six core anchors.

And the book's argument is single. The case for withdrawal is put three times, by three men, and it is put well — the world is a flood; you cannot change it; you are being mocked in the road by people who saw this before you did.

And the answer is given once, in 18.6, and it concedes everything except the conclusion: if the way prevailed in the world, I should not be trying to change it.

Then 18.8 places him among the men who withdrew, and separates him from all of them by one clause: no preconception of what may and may not be done.

Book 19 · 子张 Zi Zhang

The only book in the Analects with no the Master said in it at all. It is entirely the students — Zizhang, Zixia, Ziyou, Zengzi, Zigong — talking, disagreeing with each other, and defending their teacher.

Which makes it the best available view of what happened next. Several of them are excellent (19.3, 19.19, 19.21). Several show the tightening this book has been tracking. And two of them argue with each other in public (19.3, 19.12).

And they sign their own names. Nothing in this book is put into his mouth. When they report him, they say so: I heard from the Master.

19.1

Zizhang said: "A gentleman who, seeing danger, is ready to give his life; who, seeing gain, thinks of what is right; who in sacrifice thinks of reverence and in mourning thinks of grief — he will do."

子张曰：「士见危致命，见得思义，祭思敬，丧思哀，其可已矣。」

Non-anchor.

All four items are sound and each has support elsewhere (14.12, 3.12, 3.4). It is a four-item summary, self-attributed, with nobody in it.

19.2

Zizhang said: "Holding to virtue without breadth, believing in the way without depth — how can such a man be counted as having anything? How can he be counted as not having it?"

子张曰：「执德不弘，信道不笃，焉能为有？焉能为亡？」

Non-anchor.

The observation is real — a man whose commitments are thin is neither present nor absent. And it is a general remark with no application.

19.3

Zixia's disciples asked Zizhang about associating with others. Zizhang said: "What does Zixia say?" They replied: "Zixia says: associate with those who are fit, and reject those who are not." Zizhang said: "This differs from what I heard. The noble person honours the worthy and includes the many; he praises the good and pities the incapable. If I am greatly worthy, what can I not bear in others? If I am not worthy, others will reject me — how should I be rejecting them?"

子夏之门人问交于子张。子张曰：「子夏云何？」对曰：「子夏曰：『可者与之，其不可者拒之。』」子张曰：「异乎吾所闻：君子尊贤而容众，嘉善而矜不能。我之大贤与，于人何所不容？我之不贤与，人将拒我，如之何其拒人也？」

Anchor (core). And it is the school disagreeing with itself, on the record.

Zixia's rule is exclusive: take the fit, reject the unfit. Zizhang's answer is a better argument, and it is made in two moves.

First the positive statement: honours the worthy and includes the many; praises the good and pities the incapable. Both halves — the discrimination and the inclusion — held together.

Then the dilemma, which is the sharper part. If I am truly worthy, there is nobody I cannot bear. If I am not, then I am the one being rejected — so what am I doing rejecting? Either way, the exclusive rule has no position to be issued from.

And note the opening: this differs from what I heard. He does not attribute his version to the Master and does not claim Zixia is misquoting. He says what he heard was different — which is also the best available evidence about the exclusive reading of 1.8's have no friend who is not your equal: it was contested inside the school, and Zizhang had heard otherwise.

19.4

Zixia said: "Even in the small arts there is certainly something worth looking at. But if one goes far with them, there is the danger of being bogged down — and therefore the noble person does not take them up."

子夏曰：「虽小道，必有可观者焉；致远恐泥，是以君子不为也。」

Non-anchor — and the two halves pull apart.

The first half is generous and right: even minor skills have something in them (with 9.2, I shall take up charioteering).

The second half closes it. Therefore the noble person does not take them up. From "there is a risk" to "do not do it" — a caution converted into a prohibition.

Compare 7.6: move at ease among the arts. Not: keep clear of them.

19.5

Zixia said: "To be aware daily of what one lacks, and monthly not to forget what one is able to do — that may be called loving learning."

子夏曰：「日知其所亡，月无忘其所能，可谓好学也已矣。」

Anchor (core) — though a student's.

Two directions, and the first is the harder. Daily aware of what one does not have — that is, daily locating one's own ignorance (with 2.17: when you do not know, allow that you do not).

And the calibrated certificate — may be called — is used correctly (with 6.30, 2.11).

This is what a good student produces when he has understood the thing rather than copied the form.

19.6

Zixia said: "Learning broadly and holding firmly to one's purpose; asking with urgency and reflecting on what is at hand — ren lies therein."

子夏曰：「博学而笃志，切问而近思，仁在其中矣。」

Anchor (core).

Four items in two pairs, outward and inward: broad learning against firm purpose; urgent asking against reflection close to hand.

And lies therein is the by-product form, used correctly (with 7.15, 2.18, 13.18, 15.32): ren is not the aim being pursued; it is what appears where these four are going on.

19.7

Zixia said: "The hundred craftsmen dwell in their workshops to accomplish their work. The noble person learns in order to reach his way."

子夏曰：「百工居肆以成其事，君子学以致其道。」

Anchor (core).

居 — to dwell. The craftsman is in the workshop, daily, and that is the condition of the work getting done.

A good image, and it does the work without explanation (with 4.1's reading of dwelling, and 15.10's sharpen the tools).

19.8

Zixia said: "When the small man errs, he is sure to gloss it over."

子夏曰：「小人之过也必文。」

Anchor (core).

The distinction is not in erring but in what follows. Everyone errs (7.30, 4.7). 必文 — is sure to dress it up.

Which is exactly 15.30's placement (to err and not correct it — that is the error), and 19.21 below, from Zigong, is its positive counterpart.

19.9

Zixia said: "The noble person has three changes: seen from a distance, he is imposing; approached, he is warm; heard speaking, he is exacting."

子夏曰：「君子有三变：望之俨然，即之也温，听其言也厉。」

Non-anchor.

And the comparison with 7.37 is the reason. There: warm yet severe; commanding yet not fierce; respectful yet at ease — three pairs of qualities held together in one man at once.

Here: three appearances from three vantage points. A sequence of impressions rather than a tension sustained. The observation is fine; what was a structure has become a list.

19.10

Zixia said: "The noble person is trusted before he works the people; without trust, they take it for oppression. He is trusted before he remonstrates; without trust, it is taken for slander."

子夏曰：「君子信而后劳其民，未信则以为厉己也；信而后谏，未信则以为谤己也。」

Anchor (core).

Trust is placed before both, and in both directions — downward before employing, upward before remonstrating.

Which is 12.7 applied practically (a people without trust cannot stand), and a real insight about correction: the same words are received as counsel or as slander depending on a relation established beforehand.

19.11

Zixia said: "In great virtue, do not overstep the bounds. In small virtue, some latitude is permissible."

子夏曰：「大德不逾闲，小德出入可也。」

Anchor (core).

Not demanding everything everywhere (with 15.15, 18.10, 13.2's pardon small faults).

And what makes it work is the ordering: the great is held absolutely, which is what makes latitude in the small safe.

19.12

Ziyou said: "Zixia's disciples can sprinkle and sweep, answer and reply, and come forward and withdraw — but these are the branches. Of the root there is nothing. What is to be done?" Zixia heard it and said: "Alas! Yan You is wrong. In the way of the noble person, what is transmitted first? What is left till later, as one grows weary? It is as with plants and trees, which are distinguished by kinds. In the way of the noble person, how should there be misrepresentation? It is only the sage who has beginning and end together."

子游曰：「子夏之门人小子，当洒扫应对进退，则可矣，抑末也。本之则无，如之何？」子夏闻之曰：「噫！言游过矣！君子之道，孰先传焉？孰后倦焉？譬诸草木，区以别矣……」

Anchor (core). Two senior students arguing about pedagogy, and Zixia's reply is right.

Ziyou's objection is that the training is all surface.

And Zixia's answer is the doctrine of fitting the teaching, defended by the man being criticized: what is transmitted first? What is left till later? — there is an order, and it is not arbitrary. As with plants and trees, which are distinguished by kinds — different students, different starting points.

Which is 6.21 exactly (to those above the middling sort one may speak of higher things; to those below, one may not) and 11.22.

And the last clause is the honest one: only the sage has beginning and end together — only someone who has the whole of it could deliver it all at once. Anyone else has to sequence.

19.13

Zixia said: "Having served well, then study; having studied well, then serve."

子夏曰：「仕而优则学，学而优则仕。」

Anchor (weak) — and a reading is opposed.

优 is having surplus energy, not doing well (with 1.6's if energy remains, then study the texts). The line is a pair, and the two halves support each other: office does not end study; study leads out into use.

What we oppose: the reading that keeps only the second half — study well and take office — as the purpose of learning.

And the mechanism is simply deletion. The first clause is dropped, and what remains is the career ladder that dominated the examination system for a millennium.

Against the whole of 14.24 (in old times, men learned for themselves), 6.13 (be a ru of the noble kind, not of the small kind), and 13.5 (a man may recite the three hundred Odes and be unable to carry through a charge).

19.14

Ziyou said: "In mourning, let grief be fully reached, and stop there."

子游曰：「丧致乎哀而止。」

Anchor (core).

Six characters, and 而止 — and stop there — is the load-bearing part.

Grief is to be fully reached — not performed short. And then nothing is added: no elaboration, no display, no escalation of the observance.

Which is 3.4 (rather grief than formal correctness) and it is exactly what 11.11 records going wrong — the students who gave Yan Hui a grand funeral against his teacher's instruction.

19.15

Ziyou said: "My friend Zhang is capable of difficult things. But he is not yet ren."

子游曰：「吾友张也，为难能也，然而未仁。」

Anchor (core) — and the reading requires care.

Read as a slight, it is a student grading another student.

Read against the standard the school actually used, it is unremarkable and correct: the Master declined the word for everyone he was ever asked about (5.7, 5.19, 14.2), and declined it for himself (7.33, 14.28).

So not yet ren is not a demotion. It is the ordinary state of everybody, including the teacher. And 难能 — capable of difficult things — is real praise.

(That said: the Master suspended the judgment; Ziyou states it. The difference between "I do not know about his ren" and "he is not yet ren" is small and it is not nothing.)

19.16

Zengzi said: "How imposing Zhang is! But it is hard to practise ren alongside him."

曾子曰：「堂堂乎张也，难与并为仁矣。」

Anchor (weak).

堂堂 is genuine praise — impressive, of real presence.

And the weight is on 并 — alongside. Not: he cannot become ren. But: it is hard to work at it with him — which is an observation about collaboration, and about what an imposing presence does to the people around it.

With 19.15: two students, independently, saying the same thing about a third.

19.17

Zengzi said: "I heard from the Master: people have never fully expressed themselves. If ever, it must be in mourning a parent."

曾子曰：「吾闻诸夫子：人未有自致者也，必也亲丧乎！」

Non-anchor.

The observation is that ordinary life is conducted with the feelings held partly in check, and that one occasion breaks through.

Its failing is that it points nowhere — no judgment, no application. Reported honestly (I heard from the Master), and not opposed.

19.18

Zengzi said: "I heard from the Master: the filial conduct of Meng Zhuangzi in other respects could be matched; but not changing his father's officers or his father's government — that is hard to match."

曾子曰：「吾闻诸夫子：孟庄子之孝也，其他可能也；其不改父之臣与父之政，是难能也。」

Non-anchor.

A specific case of 1.11's three years without altering the father's way — and it is the specific form, about one man, which is how that principle survives being read as a general law.

See 1.11 for the opposition to the generalized version. Nothing is opposed here: a particular judgment about a particular succession is exactly what the general rule is not.

19.19

The Meng family appointed Yang Fu chief judge, and he consulted Zengzi. Zengzi said: "Those above have lost the way, and the people have been scattered for a long time. When you find out the facts of a case, grieve for them and take no pleasure in it."

孟氏使阳肤为士师，问于曾子。曾子曰：「上失其道，民散久矣。如得其情，则哀矜而勿喜！」

Anchor (core). The best thing anyone says in this book.

Two moves, and both are necessary.

First the cause is located above: those above have lost the way, and the people have been scattered. The crimes he is about to judge have a history, and it is not the criminals'.

Then the instruction, which is about the judge's own state: when you find out the facts, grieve — and take no pleasure in it.

And 勿喜 is the sharp word. The temptation of the office is not cruelty; it is satisfaction — the small pleasure of having got to the bottom of it, of being right about someone. That is what is forbidden.

With 12.19 (what need have you of killing?), 12.18 (if you were without desire), 13.30 (to send the people to war untaught is to throw them away).

19.20

Zigong said: "Zhou's badness was not so extreme as all that. This is why the noble person hates to dwell downstream, where all the world's foulness collects."

子贡曰：「纣之不善，不如是之甚也。是以君子恶居下流，天下之恶皆归焉。」

Anchor (core).

An unusually clear-eyed observation about how reputations work. Zhou was the last king of Yin and the standing byword for tyranny — and Zigong says the record has been loaded.

Once a name is settled as the worst, every unattributed evil flows to it. 下流 — downstream — and the image does the analysis.

Which is why the noble person hates to dwell there: not because the judgment would be unfair, but because it becomes self-feeding. (With 13.24, 15.28 on the crowd's verdicts.)

19.21

Zigong said: "The errors of the noble person are like an eclipse of sun or moon. When he errs, everyone sees it. When he corrects it, everyone looks up."

子贡曰：「君子之过也，如日月之食焉：过也，人皆见之；更也，人皆仰之。」

Anchor (core).

The image carries two things at once.

When he errs, everyone sees it — because he does not hide it (against 19.8's small man who is sure to gloss it over).

When he corrects it, everyone looks up — and the correction is more visible than the fault, and raises rather than repairs.

Which is 7.30 stated as a principle (I am fortunate; if I have a fault, others are sure to know it) and 15.30 (to err and not correct it — that is the error).

A student's line, and it belongs with the best of them.

19.22

Gongsun Chao of Wei asked Zigong: "From whom did Zhongni learn?" Zigong said: "The way of Wen and Wu has not fallen to the ground; it is in people.
