The worthy remember the greater part of it, the unworthy the lesser. There is no one who does not have something of the way of Wen and Wu. From whom would our Master not learn? And what need had he of a fixed teacher?"

卫公孙朝问于子贡曰：「仲尼焉学？」子贡曰：「文武之道，未坠于地，在人。贤者识其大者，不贤者识其小者，莫不有文武之道焉。夫子焉不学？而亦何常师之有？」

Anchor (core).

Asked to name a lineage, Zigong refuses to supply one — and the reason he gives is the important part.

It is in people. Not in texts, not in a school, not in a transmission. And everyone carries some of it — the worthy more, the unworthy less, but nobody none.

From which: from whom would he not learn? This is 7.21 as an epistemology (walking with two others, I am sure of a teacher among them).

And what need had he of a fixed teacher? — 常师 — is the direct refusal of the structure 20.1 supplies. A student, asked point-blank for a lineage, said there was not one and gave the reason.

19.23

Shusun Wushu said to the great officers at court: "Zigong is superior to Zhongni." Zifu Jingbo told Zigong. Zigong said: "Take the wall around a house as the comparison. My wall reaches only to the shoulders; one can peer over and see whatever is fine in the house. My Master's wall is many ren high. If you do not find the gate and go in, you do not see the ancestral temple in its beauty or the hundred officers in their richness. And those who find the gate are few. Is it any wonder the gentleman spoke as he did?"

叔孙武叔语大夫于朝曰：「子贡贤于仲尼。」……子贡曰：「譬之宫墙：赐之墙也及肩，窥见室家之好；夫子之墙数仞，不得其门而入，不见宗庙之美、百官之富。得其门者或寡矣。夫子之云，不亦宜乎！」

Anchor (core).

Told he is better than his teacher, Zigong makes an argument — and the last clause is what makes it a good one.

The wall image is exact: what is visible over a low wall is everything there is; what is not visible over a high one is not thereby absent.

And then: is it any wonder the gentleman spoke as he did? He does not call Shusun Wushu a fool. He says: of course he said that — the gate is hard to find. The man's error is explained rather than condemned.

19.24

Shusun Wushu spoke disparagingly of Zhongni. Zigong said: "It is no use. Zhongni cannot be disparaged. The worthiness of other men is like a hill or mound, which can be climbed over. Zhongni is the sun and moon, which cannot be climbed over. Though a man may wish to cut himself off from them, what harm does he do to the sun and moon? He only shows that he does not know his own measure."

叔孙武叔毁仲尼。子贡曰：「无以为也！仲尼不可毁也……人虽欲自绝，其何伤于日月乎？多见其不知量也。」

Anchor (core) — and this one is worth reading twice, because it is where the sanctification begins.

The argument's actual content is sound: what harm does he do to the sun and moon? Disparagement does not alter its object. The defence is that the attack is ineffective, not that it is impious.

And the imagery is a step. Hills can be climbed; the sun and moon cannot. From a man whose gate is hard to find (19.23) to a celestial body.

Which is the school's affection working, and it is understandable, and it is the material later ages built on. 9.6 records the same student calling him Heaven-endowed — and the man himself taking it apart. He is not here to take this apart.

19.25

Chen Ziqin said to Zigong: "You are being respectful. How can Zhongni be superior to you?" Zigong said: "By a single word a noble person is judged wise, and by a single word unwise. One's words must not be careless. Our Master cannot be reached, just as heaven cannot be climbed by steps. Had our Master been given a state, then, as they say: he would have raised the people, and they would have stood; led them, and they would have gone; comforted them, and they would have come; stirred them, and they would have been in harmony. In his life he was glorious; in his death he was mourned. How can he be reached?"

陈子禽谓子贡曰：「子为恭也，仲尼岂贤于子乎？」子贡曰：「君子一言以为知，一言以为不知，言不可不慎也。夫子之不可及也，犹天之不可阶而升也……」

Anchor (core) — and the book of the Analects proper ends here, with the same warmth and the same danger.

The opening rebuke is excellent: by a single word a noble person is judged wise or unwise; one's words must not be careless. Aimed at the man who has just been careless.

And the content of the praise is precise and political: raised them, and they stood; led them, and they went; comforted them, and they came; stirred them, and they were in harmony. Every clause is about what happened to people — which is the criterion this whole reading uses.

And heaven cannot be climbed by steps is a further step up from 19.24's sun and moon.

The last three chapters of this book are three defences of a dead teacher by a student who loved him — and they are the point at which a man who declined to be called a sage begins to be described as unreachable.

None of it is false. And he is not there to say does the Grand Steward know me? I was poor when I was young.

Book 19 in sum. Fifteen core anchors — a high number, from a book with no the Master said in it.

Which is the finding. The students were good. Zizhang's answer on friendship (19.3), Zengzi's instruction to the judge (19.19), Zigong on errors like eclipses (19.21) and on there being no fixed teacher (19.22) — these belong with anything in the book.

And the tightening is visible in the same pages. 19.4 turns a caution into a prohibition. 19.9 turns a sustained tension into a list of three impressions. 19.13 supplies half a line that a millennium of examinations would use.

And in the last three chapters, defending him against a slight, his ablest student calls him the sun and the moon, and then heaven itself.

He had said: I am not one who was born knowing. Nobody was left to say it for him.

Book 20 · 尧曰 Yao Yue

Three chapters, and the Analects ends.

And the three are of three different kinds: a chain of royal charges with no the Master said in it at all; a set of five virtues and four vices for a man entering government; and a three-clause summary addressed to nobody.

None of them anchors. And one of them is opposed — not for what it says, but for what it was later made to do.

20.1

Yao said: "Ah, you Shun! The succession decreed by Heaven rests on you. Hold faithfully to the centre. If the four seas fall into hardship, Heaven's gift will be ended for ever." Shun likewise charged Yu.

[Tang] said: "I, the child Lü, presume to use a black bull and to announce to the most august sovereign Lord: I dare not pardon the guilty; your servants I do not conceal — the examination of them is by your mind, O Lord. If I in my own person commit offences, they are not to be attributed to the myriad regions. If the myriad regions commit offences, the offence rests on my person."

[King Wu:] "Though I have my near relatives, they are not like men of ren. If the people transgress, let it be on me alone." … He attended to the weights and measures, examined the laws, restored offices that had lapsed, revived states that had been extinguished, restored broken lines of succession, and raised men who had withdrawn into obscurity — and the people of the empire gave their hearts to him. What he attended to: the people, food, mourning, sacrifice. Generous, he won the multitude; trustworthy, the people relied on him; diligent, he had results; just, all were pleased.

尧曰：「咨！尔舜！天之历数在尔躬，允执其中。四海困穷，天禄永终。」舜亦以命禹……「朕躬有罪，无以万方；万方有罪，罪在朕躬。」……「兴灭国，继绝世，举逸民，天下之民归心焉。」所重：民、食、丧、祭。宽则得众，信则民任焉，敏则有功，公则说。

Set aside as an editorial / canonizing layer — and one use of it is opposed.

Why it is set aside. There is no the Master said anywhere in it. It is not a record of exchange but a splice of four things: royal charges, a sacrificial prayer, an administrative programme, and a list of four virtues with their yields.

It stands at the head of the last book, and it supplies the book with a provenance. That is the character of the material, and this book records it as such — an observation about textual character, not a finding about authorship or date.

And much in it is worth keeping, which should be said plainly.

If I in my own person commit offences, they are not to be attributed to the myriad regions. If the myriad regions commit offences, the offence rests on my person. — the ruler taking the fault onto himself, which is 12.17, 12.18 and 13.6 in older language.

What he attended to: the people, food, mourning, sacrifice — and the people come first.

He revived states that had been extinguished, restored broken lines, and raised men who had withdrawn — not cutting off what had been ended; not leaving ability unused.

The layer preserved things consistent with what this reading finds.

What we oppose: reading this passage as the transmission of an exclusive lineage — such that standing in the line confers authority, and standing outside it makes one heterodox.

Why.

Because of what a lineage does to a test. Once there is a line of succession, the question shifts from is this right to who handed it down. And that runs directly against the sentence this reading treats as decisive:

"A person can enlarge the way. It is not the way that enlarges the person." （人能弘道，非道弘人。）— 15.29

The way confers no standing on those who hold it. There is no authority to be had from being in possession of it — which forecloses lineage, transmission and orthodoxy in one clause.

And the man is a poor candidate for the head of such a line. He transmitted and did not compose (7.1). He took apart "Heaven has let him loose to be a sage" when a student offered it (9.6). He declined the title (7.33). He said his way was threaded on one thing and would not say what (15.3, 4.15). And he told his students not to defer to him on what mattered most (15.36).

What we do not conclude. Transmitting an account of Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang and Wu does not by itself amount to establishing an exclusive orthodoxy. That is a framework later readers can build on this material — it is not the only thing the passage completes. And we make no finding about where the material came from or when it entered the text.

(An earlier version of this work created a special category — "borrowing the position of the book" — in order to place this chapter. That category is withdrawn. It had no boundary: on it, any preface, appendix or table of contents that shaped a reader's understanding could be judged as erecting a contrary structure. Editorial material is set aside as editorial material. No exception is needed.)

20.2

Zizhang asked Confucius: "What must one be, to be fit to govern?" The Master said: "Honour the five fine things and cast out the four bad things — then one is fit to govern." "What are the five fine things?" "The noble person is generous without expense; he works the people without their resenting it; he desires without being covetous; he is dignified without arrogance; he is imposing without fierceness." … "What are the four bad things?" "To execute without having taught — that is called cruelty. To expect completion without having given warning — that is called oppression. To be slow in issuing orders and then press for a deadline — that is called injury. And in giving what is due to anyone, to be grudging in the handing over — that is called being a petty functionary."

子张问于孔子曰：「何如斯可以从政矣？」子曰：「尊五美，屏四恶，斯可以从政矣。」……「不教而杀谓之虐；不戒视成谓之暴；慢令致期谓之贼；犹之与人也，出纳之吝，谓之有司。」

Non-anchor — and the four vices are important enough to be recorded in full.

Why it does not anchor. A numbered programme — five and four, each defined in turn, delivered as a syllabus. The address is Confucius said. And it reads as the material of governing assembled for a man about to take office, rather than as an exchange with one.

But the four vices are excellent, and three of them are this book's political argument in compressed form.

To execute without having taught — cruelty. This is 13.30 exactly (to send the people to war untaught is to throw them away): people are held to a standard they were never brought to.

To expect completion without having given warning — oppression. And to be slow with orders and then press for the deadline — injury. Both are the same fault: the governor's own failure charged to the governed.

And in giving what is due, to be grudging — that is a petty functionary. Not withholding what is owed, but handing it over meanly. The manner of giving is part of the giving (with 8.11's 吝).

The five fine things are the "…without…" form (with 7.37, 13.26, 20.2's own dignified without arrogance), and they are unobjectionable.

Nothing here is opposed. The content is sound and the form cannot carry the reconstruction.

20.3

"Without knowing what is decreed, one has no way to be a noble person. Without knowing ritual, one has no way to stand. Without knowing words, one has no way to know people."

子曰：「不知命，无以为君子也；不知礼，无以立也；不知言，无以知人也。」

Non-anchor.

All three clauses have support elsewhere. Knowing what is decreed — the boundary between what is in one's hands and what is not (14.36, 12.5, 14.35). Knowing ritual — without learning ritual, one has no footing (16.13). Knowing words — by his words you know the man (5.10, 12.20, 15.23).

Why it does not anchor: it is a summary in three clauses, with no addressee and nothing at stake — at the end of a book otherwise made of exchanges. It cannot serve as evidence of a way of teaching, because there is no one being taught in it.

A further observation, which can stand alongside without carrying the judgment. Compare 16.13's without learning ritual, one has no footing (不学礼，无以立). There the condition is a process — learning, which one is in the middle of. Here it is a state — knowing, which one either has or has not.

And a reader is being asked to establish, of himself, that he knows what is decreed, knows ritual, knows words — without being given any criterion for deciding, or any way of checking.

(An earlier version of this work opposed this chapter as erecting an entrance requirement, on the ground that it converts a process into a threshold. That is withdrawn as the deciding reason. The conditions for becoming a certain kind of person are not the same as conditions for admission to being taught — and 15.39 and 7.7 are untouched by this chapter. The process/state observation stands as an observation; the chapter does not anchor because of what it is, not because of what it does.)

Book 20 in sum. No anchors.

And the shape of the ending is worth stating.

The Analects closes with a compendium — a genealogy of kings, a programme for a man taking office, and three necessary conditions stated to nobody in particular. All three are the kinds of thing a school produces when it is consolidating.

The material this reading is built on ended earlier. A blind musician being told where the steps are (15.42). A question after a stable fire (10.17). Two opposite answers to one question, and the reason given only because a bystander was confused (11.22). A man who said: if you would feel at ease, then do it (17.21).

That gap — between the last exchange and the last chapter — is what this volume has been about.

Books 16–20: totals
Chapters100
Anchors, of which core41 / 37
Non-anchors55
Split / set aside4
Chapters with a stated opposition5 — 16.8 (two items), 16.9, 17.3, 17.25, 19.13, 20.1

Three things this table shows.

The proportion inverts here. In Books 11–15, 115 of 166 chapters were core anchors. Here, 55 of 100 do not anchor at all — and the character of the material is the reason: numbered sets, rosters, historical commentary, ritual protocol, and a canonizing frame.

Almost none of it is opposed. Five chapters carry a stated opposition, out of a hundred — and of the fifty-five non-anchors, the overwhelming majority carry no criticism whatsoever. The three friendships, the nine considerations, the five fine things and four bad things: sound material, with no one in it.

And Book 19 is the exception that matters. Fifteen core anchors, in a book with no the Master said in it at all. The students were good — and the tightening is visible in the same pages.

A closing note on this volume

Four things are opposed in this work, and the passages are countable on two hands. Six passages, out of roughly five hundred.

And all four are the same operation: taking something that was alive — fitted to a person, revisable, dependent on the other party's own judgment — and fixing it: as a rank, an authority, a lineage, a boundary.

What is opposed is that operation. Not the content of any one passage, and never the passage's presence in the book.

The Analects is not, on this reading, a text with doctrine in it that must be extracted. It is a record that mostly runs the other way, in which a very small number of passages were later made to carry an enormous amount.

Reading the Analects Through Self-as-an-End · Han Qin (秦汉) · ORCID 0009-0009-9583-0018 · self-as-an-end.net · CC BY 4.0
